of projective approximations
in
theorem

3.27
.

Definition 3.15
.

Given
\Gamma,\Pi\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
,
we say that
\Pi
is

\Gamma
-projective resolution for
A
if

•

\Pi
is a set of independent propositions, i.e. for

B,C\in\Pi
,
\vdash B\to C
implies
B=C
.

•

Every
B\in\Pi
is
\Gamma
-projective.

•

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${\Gamma}$}\end{picture}}}\bigvee\Pi
.

•

\vdash\bigvee\Pi\to A
.

A
\{\top\}
-projective resolution is also called
projective resolution.

Note that
\emptyset
is a projective resolution for
a proposition which is not unifiable.
The greatest lower bound (glb) for a proposition
A
is defined in
section

2.7
. Intuitively
a glb for
A
w.r.t.
(\Gamma,{\sf T})
is the best

\Gamma
-approximation from below inside the logic
{\sf T}
.

Remark 3.16
.

If
\Pi
is
\Gamma
-projective resolution of
A
then

\bigvee\Pi
is a glb for
A
w.r.t.
({{\downarrow}\Gamma^{\vee}},{\sf IPC})
.

Proof.

By
theorem

2.2
we have
A\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${{\sf T}}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}\Gamma}}$}\end{picture}}\bigvee\Pi
and hence by
theorem

2.5
we have
A\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${{\sf T}}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}\Gamma}^{\!\vee}}$}\end{picture}}\bigvee\Pi
.
Thus

theorem

2.6
implies desired result.
∎

Theorem 3.17
.

Whenever
{\sf par}=\emptyset
,
every
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
has projective
resolution.

Proof.

See
[
Ghilardi, 1999
, theorem 5]
.
We will also prove a generalization of this
result in
theorem

3.27
.
∎

First some preliminary definitions. We refer the reader
to
[
Ghilardi, 1999
]
for more information on these notions.

Let

\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(W^{\prime},\preccurlyeq^{\prime},\mathrel{V}^{\prime})

are two Kripke models with the roots

w_{0}
and
w^{\prime}_{0}
. Also let

\mathcal{K}(w):=\{a\in{\sf atom}:\mathcal{K},w\Vdash a\}
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathcal{K})
be defined as
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bigcup_{w\in W}\mathcal{K}(w))
.
We say that
\mathcal{K}
is with finite valuations if for every

w\in W
we have
\mathcal{K}(w)
is finite. Also define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\sim_{0}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\quad

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad\mathcal{K}(w_{0})=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(w_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\sim_{n+1}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\quad

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad\forall\,w\in W\,\exists\,w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}_{w}\sim_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}})\text{ and vice versa}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\leq_{0}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\quad

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad\mathcal{K}(w_{0})\supseteq\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(w^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\leq_{n+1}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\quad

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad\forall\,w\in W\,\exists\,w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}(\mathcal{K}_{w}\sim_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}})

Evidently
\sim_{n}
is an equivalence relation and

\leq_{n}
is reflexive transitive. One may easily
observe by induction on
n
that
\mathcal{K}\sim_{n+1}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}

implies
\mathcal{K}\sim_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. Hence

\mathcal{K}\sim_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
(
\mathcal{K}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
) implies

\mathcal{K}\sim_{m}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
(
\mathcal{K}\leq_{m}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
) for every
m\leq n
.

Let
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)
indicate the maximum number of nested implications in
A
:

•

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(a)=c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(\top)=c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(\bot)=0
for atomic
a
.

•

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A\circ B):=\max\{c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A),c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(B)\}
, for
\circ\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
.

•

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A\to B):=1+\max\{c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A),c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(B)\}
.

Remember that by default we assume the set
{\sf atom}
to be a finite set.

Remark 3.18
.

Modulo
{\sf IPC}
-provable equivalence relation, there are finitely many
propositions
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
with
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)\leq n
.

Proof.

By induction on
n
, we define an upper bound
f(n)

for the number of propositions
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
with

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)\leq n
.

1.

f(0):
Observe that any
A
with
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)=0
is
{\sf IPC}
-equivalent to a disjunction of conjunctions of atomics.
Hence
f(0)=2^{2^{m}}
is an obvious upper bound,
in which
m
is the number of atomics in
{\sf atom}
.

2.

f(n+1):
For every implication
B\to C

with
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(B\to C)\leq n+1
, we have
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(B),c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(C)\leq n
,
and hence
f(n)^{2}
is an upper bound for the number of
inequivalent such propositions. Then since
modulo
{\sf IPC}
-provable equivalence every proposition is a disjunction of conjunctions of atomics or implications, the following definition is an upperbound:

f(n+1):=2^{2^{[m+f(n)^{2}]}}.\qed

Lemma 3.19
.

Every
A\in{\sf NNIL}
has an
{\sf IPC}
-provable equivalent

A^{\prime}\in{\sf NNIL}
with
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A^{\prime})\leq\#{\sf atom}
.

Proof.

Observe that every
A\in{\sf NNIL}
has an
{\sf IPC}
-equivalent
B\in{\sf NNIL}

such that
B=\bigwedge_{i}\bigvee_{j}C_{i}^{j}

and every implication in

C^{j}_{i}
is of the form
a\to E
, with
a\in{\sf atom}
and

E
does not contain
a
. Then one may easily prove the statement of this lemma by induction on the number of elements in
{\sf atom}
.
∎

Lemma 3.20
.

For every Kripke model
\mathcal{K}
,
there exists a proposition
{[\![{\mathcal{K}}]\!]_{n}}\in\mathcal{L}_{0}

with the following properties:

•

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash{[\![{\mathcal{K}}]\!]_{n}}
iff
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}
.

•

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}({[\![{\mathcal{K}}]\!]_{n}})\leq n
.

Proof.

We only give the definition of
A
here, and refer the
reader to
[
Ghilardi, 1999
, proposition 1]
for its proof.

Let

\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})

and define
{[\![{\mathcal{K}}]\!]_{0}}:=\bigwedge\mathcal{K}(w_{0})
and

{[\![{\mathcal{K}}]\!]_{n+1}}:=\bigwedge_{\{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}:\forall\,w\in W(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\nsim_{n}\mathcal{K}_{w})\}}\left({[\![{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}]\!]_{n}}\to\bigvee_{\{\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}:\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\nleq_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\}}{[\![{\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}}]\!]_{n}}\right).\qed

Corollary 3.21
.

For every Kripke models
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
,
we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}
iff
for every
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
with
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)\leq n
we have

\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
implies
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash A
.

Proof.

For the left to right direction, use induction on
n
.
For the right to left, if
\mathcal{K}
is with finite
valuations, one may easily use
section

3.4
and have desired result. Otherwise One may restrict
\mathcal{K}
and

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
to arbitrary finite atomics and then apply previous argument.
∎

Corollary 3.22
.

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\sim_{n}\mathcal{K}
iff for every
A
with
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)\leq n

we have

\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
iff
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash A
.

Proof.

First observe that
\mathcal{K}\sim_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is equivalent to

\mathcal{K}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}
and then use

section

3.4
.
∎

Lemma 3.23
.

A class
\mathscr{K}
of Kripke models
is of the form
\textup{Mod}(A)
with
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)\leq n
, iff
\mathscr{K}
is
\leq_{n}
-downward
closed, i.e. for every Kripke model
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}

with

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{K}
we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}
.

Proof.

For the left-to-right direction, let
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}\in\textup{Mod}(A)
for some
A
with
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)\leq n
. Since

\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
,
section

3.4
implies

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash A
and hence
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\textup{Mod}(A)
.

For the other direction, let
\mathscr{K}
is
\leq_{n}
-downward
closed and define

A:=\bigvee_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{K}}{[\![{\mathcal{K}}]\!]_{n}}.

By

section

3.4
, the disjunction is finite
and hence
A
is indeed a proposition.
One may easily observe that
section

3.4
implies that

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)\leq n
and

\mathscr{K}=\textup{Mod}(A)
.
∎

Lemma 3.24
.

If a class of Kripke models
\mathscr{K}
is
{\sf par}
-extendible and
\theta

is a substitution, then
\theta(\mathscr{K})

is also
{\sf par}
-extendible.

Proof.

Easy and left to the reader.
∎

We say that a class
\mathscr{K}
of Kripke models
is stable, if for every
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{K}
and every node

w
in
\mathcal{K}
we have
\mathcal{K}_{w}\in\mathscr{K}
.

Remark 3.25
.

A class
\mathscr{K}
of Kripke models
is
{\sf par}
-extendible iff for
every finite stable
class of models
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
which is
{\sf par}
-submodel of
some
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{K}
, a
{\sf par}
-variant of

\sum(\mathscr{K}^{\prime},\mathcal{K})
belongs to
\mathscr{K}
.

Proof.

Easy and left to the reader.
∎

Define

\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}:=\{\mathcal{K}:\exists\,\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}\ (\mathcal{K}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime})\text{ and }\mathcal{K}\text{ is a Kripke model}\}
.

Lemma 3.26
.

If
\mathscr{K}
is
{\sf par}
-extendible
stable class of Kripke models, then so is

\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
, for every
n>\#{\sf par}
.
(
\#{\sf par}
indicates the number of elements in
{\sf par}
.
)

Proof.

We only prove here the
{\sf par}
-extendibility
of
\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
and leave other properties to the reader.

Let
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}=\{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i}
is a finite set of models
in
\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
,
which are
{\sf par}
-submodels of some
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
.
By
section

3.4
we may also assume
that
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
is stable.
We must show that a
{\sf par}
-variant of

\sum(\mathscr{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
belongs to
\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
.
Since
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
and
\mathscr{K}
is stable,
there is some

\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{K}
such that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\sim_{n-1}\mathcal{K}
.
Similarly, since
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}\in\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
, there is some

\mathcal{K}_{i}\in\mathscr{K}

such that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}\sim_{n-1}\mathcal{K}_{i}
. Let

\mathscr{F}:=\{\mathcal{K}_{i}\}_{i}
.

First we show that
\mathscr{F}
is a

{\sf par}
-submodel of
\mathcal{K}
.
Since
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
is a
{\sf par}
-submodel of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
,
by
theorem

3.7

we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}\Vdash[\![{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}]\!]_{{}_{{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}
.
From
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}\sim_{n-1}\mathcal{K}_{i}
,
sections

3.4
and
3.4
we get

\mathcal{K}_{i}\Vdash[\![{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}]\!]_{{}_{{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}
. Also
since
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\sim_{n-1}\mathcal{K}
, by

sections

3.4
and
3.4
we have

[\![{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}]\!]_{{}_{{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}=[\![{\mathcal{K}}]\!]_{{}_{{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}
. Hence
\mathcal{K}_{i}\Vdash[\![{\mathcal{K}}]\!]_{{}_{{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}
, and by
theorem

3.7

we have
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a
{\sf par}
-submodel of

\mathcal{K}
. Hence
\mathscr{F}
is a
{\sf par}
-submodel of

\mathcal{K}
.

We go back to the main proof.
Since
\mathscr{F}
is

{\sf par}
-submodel of
\mathcal{K}
, by extendibility
of
\mathscr{K}
, there exist a
{\sf par}
-variant
\hat{\mathcal{K}}

of
\sum(\mathscr{F},\mathcal{K})
in
\mathscr{K}
.
Let
w_{0}
is the root of
\mathcal{K}

which is also the root of
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
and

w^{\prime}_{0}
is the root of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.
Define the
{\sf par}
-variant
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
of

\sum(\mathscr{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})

for atomic
x\not\in{\sf par}
as follows:

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},w^{\prime}_{0}\Vdash x\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\hat{\mathcal{K}},w_{0}\Vdash x.

It is enough to show that
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}\in\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
.
For this aim it is enough to show

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}\leq_{n}\hat{\mathcal{K}}
. From definition of

\hat{\mathcal{K}}
and
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
, it is clear that
it is enough to show that
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}\sim_{n-1}\hat{\mathcal{K}}
.
We use induction on

k\leq n-1
and show
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}\sim_{k}\hat{\mathcal{K}}
.

If
k=0
, we must show that for every atomic
a

we have

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},w^{\prime}_{0}\Vdash a\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\hat{\mathcal{K}},w_{0}\Vdash a.

For atomic variables
x
, by definition of
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
, we already have this.
Also since
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
is a
{\sf par}
-variant of

\sum(\mathscr{F},\mathcal{K})
,

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
is a
{\sf par}
-variant of

\sum(\mathscr{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{K}\sim_{0}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
,
for every
p\in{\sf par}
we also have

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},w^{\prime}_{0}\Vdash p\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\hat{\mathcal{K}},w_{0}\Vdash p.

Then let
0<k<n
and show
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}\sim_{k}\hat{\mathcal{K}}
.
We have the following items to prove:

•

For every node
w^{\prime}
in
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
, there is some

w
in
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
such that
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}}\sim_{k-1}\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{w}
. If
w^{\prime}
is the root of
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
,
take
w
also the root of
\hat{\mathcal{K}}

and we have desired result by
induction hypothesis. If
w^{\prime}
is not the root of

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
, since
\mathscr{F}^{\prime}
is stable, we may let

w^{\prime}
as a root
w^{\prime}_{i}
of some
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}
.
Take
w=w_{i}
.
Then by definition of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
, we have

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}}=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}\sim_{n-1}\mathcal{K}_{i}=\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{w}

Since
k-1\leq n-1
, we have the desired result.

•

For every node
w
in
\hat{\mathcal{K}}
, there is some

w^{\prime}
in
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
such that
\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}}\sim_{k-1}\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{w}
. Again if
w
is the root, take
w^{\prime}

also the root and we are done by induction hypothesis.
If
w
is not the root, there is some
i
such that

w
is a node of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
. Since

\mathcal{K}_{i}\sim_{n-1}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}
, there is some
w^{\prime}
in

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i}
such that

(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i})_{w^{\prime}}\sim_{n-2}(\mathcal{K}_{i})_{w}
. Since

k-1\leq n-2
, we have

\hat{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{w^{\prime}}=(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{i})_{w^{\prime}}\sim_{k-1}(\mathcal{K}_{i})_{w}=\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{w},

as desired.∎

Theorem 3.27
.

Every
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
has

{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-projective resolution
\Pi
.
Moreover for every
B\in\Pi
we have
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(B)\leq\max\{c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A),1+\#{\sf par}\}
and
\Pi
is a computable
function of
A
.

Proof.

Given a substitution
\theta

and
A^{\prime}\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
such that

\vdash A^{\prime}\to\theta(A)
,
we will find some
B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
,
with the following properties:

1.

B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}
is
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-projective.

2.

\vdash A^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\theta(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})
.

3.

\vdash B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}\to A
.

4.

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})\leq n
for
n:=\max\{c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A),1+\#{\sf par}\}
(
\#{\sf par}
indicates the number of atomics in
{\sf par}
).

Then by items 1-3 (an independent subset of) the following set is a

{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-projective resolution for
A
:

\Pi:=\{B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}:A^{\prime}\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}\text{ and $\theta$ a substitution such that }\vdash A^{\prime}\to\theta(A)\}.

Moreover
section

3.4
and
item (4) implies that
\Pi
is finite, as desired.
So it remains to find
B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}

with mentioned properties.
Define

\mathscr{K}:=\theta(\textup{Mod}(A^{\prime})):=\{\theta(\mathcal{K}):\mathcal{K}\in\textup{Mod}(A^{\prime})\}

Since
\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
has downward
\leq_{n}
-closure condition,
we may apply
section

3.4

and find some proposition, e.g.
B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}
,
such that
c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})\leq n
(so item 4 is satisfied)
and
\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}=\textup{Mod}(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})
.
Since
A^{\prime}\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
, evidently it is

{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-projective. Hence by
theorem

3.12
,

A^{\prime}
is
{\sf par}
-extendible.
Hence by
section

3.4
,

\mathscr{K}
is
{\sf par}
-extendible.
Since
\mathscr{K}
is stable
and
n>\#{\sf par}
,
Section

3.4

implies that
\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
is also
{\sf par}
-extendible.
Hence
B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}
is
{\sf par}
-extendible and by

theorem

3.12
,
B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}
is
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-projective.
So item (1) is satisfied.

To show item 3 for
B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}
,
it is enough to show
\mathcal{K}\Vdash B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}\to A
for every finite rooted model
\mathcal{K}
. If
\mathcal{K}\Vdash B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}
, we have
\mathcal{K}\in\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
. Hence
\mathcal{K}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
for
some
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}
. Then
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\theta(\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime})

for some finite rooted
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}
such that

\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\Vdash A^{\prime}
. Since
\vdash A^{\prime}\to\theta(A)
,
we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\Vdash\theta(A)
, and by

section

3.2
we get
\theta(\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime})\Vdash A
,
whence
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash A
. Since

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A)\leq n
and
\mathcal{K}\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
,

section

3.4
implies that

\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
, as desired.

It remains to show that item 2 holds.
It is enough to show
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\theta(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})
for arbitrary finite rooted
\mathcal{K}
.
If
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A^{\prime}
, then
\theta(\mathcal{K})\Vdash A^{\prime}
and
hence
\theta(\mathcal{K})\in\mathscr{K}\subseteq\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}=\textup{Mod}(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})
.
Then
\theta(\mathcal{K})\Vdash B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}
and hence
\mathcal{K}\Vdash\theta(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})
. For the other direction, let

\mathcal{K}\Vdash\theta(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})
. Hence
\theta(\mathcal{K})\Vdash B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta}
and then
\theta(\mathcal{K})\in\textup{Mod}(B^{A^{\prime}}_{\theta})=\langle\mathscr{K}\rangle_{n}
.
So there is some
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}
such that

\theta(\mathcal{K})\leq_{n}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{K}
,
there is some
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\theta(\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime})
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\Vdash A^{\prime}
. Since

A^{\prime}=\theta(A^{\prime})
, we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\Vdash\theta(A^{\prime})

and hence
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash A^{\prime}
. By

section

3.4

c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A^{\prime})<n
and the

section

3.4
implies
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A^{\prime}
.

Finally we provide an algorithm which computes

\Pi
. Given
A
, compute the finite set

\Pi^{\prime}:=\{B\in\mathcal{L}_{0}:c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(B)\leq\max\{c_{\!{}_{\to}\!}(A),1+\#{\sf par}\}\text{ and }\vdash B\to A\text{ and }B\in{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}\}.

Note that
\Pi^{\prime}
is computable since
{\sf IPC}
is decidable and
by
section

3.3
we can decide

B\in{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}
. Finally one may easily find
\Pi\subseteq\Pi^{\prime}

which includes pairwise
{\sf IPC}
-independent propositions, as required
for projective resolutions.
∎

3.5
Projective resolution for
NNIL

In this subsection, we will see that
projective resolution of
a
{\sf NNIL}
-proposition gets a more elegant form.
We will use this form later for characterization of

{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-admissible rules of
{\sf IPC}
, specifically
for the validity of disjunction rule.
By
theorem

3.17
or equivalently

theorem

3.27
with empty
{\sf par}
,
there is a finite projective resolution for every proposition
A
, i.e. a set
\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\}
,
with the following properties:

•

Every unifier of
A
, is also a unifier of some

A_{i}
, in other words
A\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!\bigvee A_{i}
.

•

\vdash\bigvee A_{i}\to A
.

•

A_{i}
is projective for every
i\leq n
.

We will prove here that if
A\in{\sf NNIL}
, the projective
resolution can be chosen such that
every
A_{i}
is
{\sf NNIL}
and moreover
\vdash A\leftrightarrow\bigvee A_{i}
.

Given
A\in{\sf NNIL}
, we say that
A
is a
{\sf T}
-component
if
A=\bigwedge\Gamma\wedge\bigwedge\Delta

with the following properties:

•

Every
B\in\Gamma
is atomic.

•

Every
B\in\Delta
is an implication

C\to D
for some atomic
C
such that

{\sf T}\nvdash\bigwedge\Gamma\to C
.

Lemma 3.28
.

Let
{\sf T}
be a logic extending
{\sf IPC}
.
Every
A\in{\sf NNIL}
can be decomposed to
{\sf T}
-components,
i.e. there is a finite set of

{\sf T}
-components
\Gamma_{A}
such that

{\sf T}\vdash A\leftrightarrow\bigvee\Gamma_{A}
. Moreover,
if
A\in{\sf NNIL}({\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle a\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle a\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle a\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle a\hfil$\crcr}}}})
then
\Gamma_{A}\subseteq{\sf NNIL}({\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle a\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle a\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle a\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle a\hfil$\crcr}}}})
.

Proof.

We use induction on

{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A)
(the set of atomic
formulas in
A
) ordered by
\supset
and find some finite set
\Gamma_{A}
of
{\sf T}
-components with

{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(\Gamma_{A})\subseteq{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A)
and
{\sf T}\vdash\bigvee\Gamma_{A}\leftrightarrow A
.
As induction hypothesis assume that for every
{\sf T}
and

B\in{\sf NNIL}
with

{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(B)\subset{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A)
there is a finite set

\Gamma_{B}
of

{\sf T}
-components such that
{\sf T}\vdash B\leftrightarrow\bigvee\Gamma

and
{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(\Gamma_{B})\subseteq{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(B)
.
For the induction step, assume that
A\in{\sf NNIL}
is given.
Using derivation in
{\sf IPC}
one may easily find finite sets

\Gamma_{i}
and
\Delta_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
such that

•

{\sf IPC}\vdash A\leftrightarrow\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
,
in which
A_{i}:=\bigwedge\Gamma_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\Delta_{i}
.

•

\Delta_{i}
includes only atomic propositions.

•

\Gamma_{i}
includes implications
with atomic antecedents.

•

{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(\Gamma_{i}\cup\Delta_{i})\subseteq{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A)
.

It is enough to decompose every
A_{i}
to
{\sf T}
-components.
If
{\sf T}\nvdash\bigwedge\Delta_{i}\to E
for every antecedent

E
of an implication in
\Gamma_{i}
, then
A_{i}
already is a

{\sf T}
-component and we are done. Otherwise, there is some

E\to F\in\Gamma_{i}
such that
{\sf T}\vdash\bigwedge\Delta_{i}\to E
.
Then let
A_{i}^{\prime}:=A_{i}[E:\top]
, i.e. the replacement of every
occurrences of
E
in
A_{i}
with
\top
. Also let
{\sf T}^{\prime}:={\sf T}+E
.
Hence
{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A^{\prime}_{i})\subsetneqq{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A)
and by induction hypothesis
we may decompose
A^{\prime}_{i}
to
{\sf T}^{\prime}
-components:

{\sf T},E\vdash A^{\prime}_{i}\leftrightarrow\bigvee_{j}B_{j}

It is not difficult to observe that if
B_{j}
is a
{\sf T}^{\prime}
-component then

B^{\prime}_{j}:=E\wedge B_{j}
is a
{\sf T}
-component. Moreover

{\sf T}\vdash E\wedge A^{\prime}_{i}\leftrightarrow\bigvee_{j}B^{\prime}_{j}
and since

{\sf IPC}\vdash(E\wedge A^{\prime}_{i})\leftrightarrow(E\wedge A_{i})
and
{\sf T}\vdash A_{i}\to E
,
we get

{\sf T}\vdash A_{i}\leftrightarrow\bigvee_{j}B^{\prime}_{j}

Hence we have decomposed
A_{i}
to
{\sf T}
-components
B^{\prime}_{j}
with

{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(B^{\prime}_{j})\subseteq{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A)
, as desired.
∎

Lemma 3.29
.

Every
{\sf IPC}
-component is extendible.

Proof.

Let
B=\bigwedge B_{i}
is an
{\sf IPC}
-component and

\mathscr{K}
be a finite class of finite rooted Kripke models
for
B
. We must show that a variant of

\sum(\mathscr{K})
is a model of
B
.
Let
w_{0}
be the root of
\sum(\mathscr{K})
and
define a variant
\mathcal{K}
of
\sum(\mathscr{K})
as follows.

\mathcal{K},w_{0}\Vdash a
iff
a=B_{i}
for some
i
.
Then it is easy to observe that

\mathcal{K},w_{0}\Vdash B
.
∎

Corollary 3.30
.

For
A\in{\sf NNIL}
there is a finite set
\Delta

of projective
and
{\sf NNIL}
propositions with

\vdash A\leftrightarrow\bigvee\Delta.

Proof.

Use
sections

3.5
,
3.5
and
3.10
.
∎

Lemma 3.31
.

Every extendible
A
is
{\sf IPC}
-prime, i.e. if

\vdash A\to(B\vee C)
, then
either
\vdash A\to B
or
\vdash A\to C
.

Proof.

We prove this by contraposition. Let
\nvdash A\to B

and
\nvdash A\to C
. Then there are some Kripke
models
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{2}
such that

\mathcal{K}_{1}\Vdash A
,
\mathcal{K}_{1}\nVdash B
,

\mathcal{K}_{2}\Vdash A
and
\mathcal{K}_{2}\nVdash B
. Since

A
is extendible, there is some variant

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
of
\sum(\{\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2}\})

such that
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
.
Since

\mathcal{K}_{1}\nVdash B
we have
\mathcal{K}\nVdash B
. Similarly

\mathcal{K}\nVdash C
. Hence
\mathcal{K}\nVdash B\vee C

and then
\mathcal{K}\nVdash A\to(B\vee C)
.
∎

Theorem 3.32
.

Given
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
, the following are equivalent:

1.

A
is an
{\sf IPC}
-component,
(
modulo
{\sf IPC}
-provable equivalence relation
)

2.

A
is extendible,

3.

A
is
{\sf IPC}
-prime.

Proof.

1\Rightarrow 2
:
section

3.5
.

2\Rightarrow 3
:
section

3.5
.

3\Rightarrow 1
: Let
A
is
{\sf IPC}
-prime.
By
section

3.5
it can be decomposed to

{\sf IPC}
-components
\Gamma_{A}
.
Thus
\vdash A\leftrightarrow\bigvee\Gamma_{A}
and by
{\sf IPC}
-primality of
A

we have
\vdash A\to B
for some
B\in\Gamma_{A}
. Then
\vdash A\leftrightarrow B

and hence
A
is
{\sf IPC}
-equivalent to some
{\sf IPC}
-component.
∎

Remember that
{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
indicates the set of
{\sf IPC}
-prime
and
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-propositions.

Corollary 3.33
.

Up to
{\sf IPC}
-provable equivalence relation, we have

{\sf NNIL}={\sf P}{\sf NNIL}^{\vee}
and
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}={\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})^{\vee}
.

Proof.

By
section

3.5
every
A\in{\sf NNIL}
can be decomposed to

{\sf IPC}
-components
\Gamma_{A}
such that

A\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
implies

\Gamma_{A}\subseteq{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
. Then
theorem

3.32

implies that every
E\in\Gamma_{A}
is
{\sf IPC}
-prime. Hence

\bigvee\Gamma_{A}\in{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}^{\vee}
and moreover

A\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
implies
\bigvee\Gamma_{A}\in{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})^{\vee}
.
∎

Corollary 3.34
.

{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${{\sf IPC}}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ \ \ }={\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${{\sf IPC}}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }
.

Proof.

Sections

3.5
and
2.5
.
∎

A consequence of the results in this subsection is that now
we have uniqueness of the projective resolutions:

Theorem 3.35

(
Projective Resolution).

Every
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
has a
{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
-projective resolution.
Moreover this resolution is computable and
unique up to
{\sf IPC}
-provable equivalency,
i.e. for every two
{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
-projective resolutions

\Delta=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}\}
and
\Delta^{\prime}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}\}

for
A
, we have

m=n
and there is some permutation
\sigma
such that
for every
i
,
\vdash B_{i}\leftrightarrow C_{\sigma(i)}
.

Proof.

Given
A
, by
theorem

3.27
there is a

{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-projective resolution
\Delta
for
A
. Then define

\Pi_{0}:=\{E\wedge E^{\prime}:E\in\Delta\text{ and }E^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{E^{\dagger}}\}

in which
E^{\dagger}\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
is the
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-projection of
E

and
\Gamma_{E^{\dagger}}
is the decomposition of
E^{\dagger}
to

{\sf IPC}
-components, as provided by
section

3.5
. Finally
let
\Pi\subseteq\Pi_{0}
be some
\subseteq
-minimal set with

\vdash\bigvee\Pi_{0}\leftrightarrow\bigvee\Pi
.
Then by

section

3.5
and the following fact
one may easily observe that
\Pi
is a

{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
-projective resolution for
A
: if
A\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.38902pt\hskip-6.38902pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 6.389pt\hskip-6.389pt\hbox{${\rightarrowtail}\!\!\!\!\!{\twoheadrightarrow}$}\hskip-6.389pt\hskip-1.9045pt\raisebox{5.22394pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}\hskip-1.9045pt\hskip 6.389pt}$}\hskip-6.38902pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.13539pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 6.38902pt}}A^{\prime}\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
and
E\in{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
, then
(A\wedge E)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.38902pt\hskip-6.38902pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 6.389pt\hskip-6.389pt\hbox{${\rightarrowtail}\!\!\!\!\!{\twoheadrightarrow}$}\hskip-6.389pt\hskip-1.9045pt\raisebox{5.22394pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}\hskip-1.9045pt\hskip 6.389pt}$}\hskip-6.38902pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.13539pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 6.38902pt}}(A^{\prime}\wedge E)
.

For the uniqueness, it is enough to show that for every

{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
-projective
E
, if

E\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }\bigvee_{i}F_{i}

then for some
i
we have

\vdash E\to F_{i}
. Let
E\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 6.38902pt\hskip-6.38902pt\hbox{$\hbox{\hskip 6.389pt\hskip-6.389pt\hbox{${\rightarrowtail}\!\!\!\!\!{\twoheadrightarrow}$}\hskip-6.389pt\hskip-1.9045pt\raisebox{5.22394pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\theta$}}\hskip-1.9045pt\hskip 6.389pt}$}\hskip-6.38902pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.13539pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 6.38902pt}}E^{\dagger}
.
Then by
E\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }\bigvee_{i}F_{i}
we have

\vdash\theta(E^{\dagger}\to\bigvee_{i}F_{i})
.
Hence
\vdash E^{\dagger}\to\bigvee_{i}\theta(F_{i})
and
since
E^{\dagger}
is
{\sf IPC}
-prime, we have

\vdash E^{\dagger}\to\theta(F_{i})
for some
i
.
Thus
section

3.1
implies
\vdash E\to F_{i}
, as desired.
∎

4
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-admissible rules of
{\sf IPC}

In
[
Iemhoff, 2001b
]
,
the admissibility relation
\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!
is characterized by means of preservation relation
\rhd
and its Kripke semantics, called

{\sf AR}
-models. In this section we will characterize
and prove the decidability of

\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }
, the
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-admissible rules of
{\sf IPC}

(see
section

2.6
).
For this end, we imitate the route in

[
Iemhoff, 2001b
]
, i.e. we define a system
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
for the

{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-admissible rules of
{\sf IPC}
and also introduce
a Kripke semantic for it and prove the soundness and completeness.
Finally using this and the results
in
section

3
we prove that
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
is sound and complete for
both
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-admissibility and
{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}}
-preservativity,
i.e.
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
iff
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
iff
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
.

4.1
The system
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
is a system which proves propositions
in the form
A\rhd B
, and
A,B\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
.
Before we continue with the axioms and rules of the
system
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
, let us first define a notation.

\{A\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(B):=\begin{cases}B\quad&:B\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}\\
A\to B&:\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Then
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
is defined as
[{\sf IPC}]
(as defined in
section

2.6
) plus the following axiom and rule:

{\sf V}^{\sf par}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}:

B\to C\rhd\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})
, in which

B=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(E_{i}\to F_{i})
and

C=\bigvee_{i=n+1}^{n+m}E_{i}
.

A\rhd B

(
p\in{\sf par}
)

\textup{Mont}({\sf par})

p\to A\rhd p\to B

Remark 4.1
.

The system
{\sf AR}
, as defined in
[
Iemhoff, 2001b
]
,
is
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
with
{\sf par}=\emptyset
. The Visser rule
{\sf V}^{\sf par}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}
in this case
is proved to be of central importance
[
Iemhoff, 2005
]
.

Remark 4.2
.

As we will see in
section

4.3
,
the following extension of the Montagna’s rule is
admissible in
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
:

A\rhd B

(
E\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
)

.

E\to A\rhd E\to B

Remark 4.3
.

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
is closed under general substitutions
\theta
with

\theta(p)\in\{\top,\bot\}\cup{\sf par}
for every
p\in{\sf par}
, i.e.
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
implies
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash\theta(A)\rhd\theta(B)
.

Proof.

Use induction on the complexity of proof
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
. All cases are easy and left to the reader.
∎

The following theorem is from
[
Iemhoff, 2001b
]
.

Theorem 4.4
.

A\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!B
iff
{\sf AR}\vdash A\rhd B
.

Lemma 4.5
.

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
implies
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
.

Proof.

We use induction on the complexity of the proof

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
. All cases are easy except for
the axiom
{\sf V}^{\sf par}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}
and the rules
{\textup{Mont}({\sf par})}
and Disj.

•

{\sf V}^{\sf par}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}
:
Let
C=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(E_{i}\to F_{i})
and
D=\bigvee_{i=n+1}^{n+m}E_{i}
.
We show
C\to D\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{C\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})
.
So assume that
\theta
is a substitution
and
G\in{{\sf NNIL}({\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}})}
and show that

\vdash G\to\theta(C)
implies

\vdash G\to\theta(\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{C\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i}))
.
We reason by contraposition.
Let
\nvdash G\to\theta(\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{C\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i}))
.
Hence for every
i\leq n+m
we have

\nvdash\theta(G\to\{C\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i}))
. Then
for every
i
there is some Kripke model
\mathcal{K}_{i}

with the root
w_{i}

such that
\mathcal{K}_{i}\Vdash G
and

\mathcal{K}_{i}\nVdash\theta({E_{i}})
and
moreover for every
i

with
E_{i}\not\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}

we have
\mathcal{K}_{i}\Vdash C
. Since
G
is projective,
by
theorem

3.10
it is extendible.
Let
\mathcal{K}
be a variant of
\sum(\{\mathcal{K}_{i}\}_{i})

such that
\mathcal{K}\Vdash G
and
w_{0}
be its root.
Also define
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
as follows:
for every
i
such that
E_{i}\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}
,
eliminate
\mathcal{K}_{i}
(we mean its nodes) from
\mathcal{K}
.
Then evidently
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is a submodel of
\mathcal{K}
.
Since
G
is
{\sf NNIL}
, by
theorem

3.5
,
we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash G
.
It is enough to show that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash\theta(C)

and

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\nVdash\theta(D)
. Since for every node
w
in

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
other than
w_{0}
, we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w\Vdash\theta(C)
,
if we show
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w_{0}\nVdash\theta(E_{i})
for every
i
,
we have both
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Vdash\theta(C)

and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\nVdash\theta(D)
and we are done.
We have two cases. (1)
E_{i}\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}
.
In this case we have
\theta(E_{i})=E_{i}
, and since

\mathcal{K},w_{i}\nVdash E_{i}
we have
\mathcal{K},w_{0}\nVdash E_{i}

and hence
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w_{0}\nVdash E_{i}
. (2)

E_{i}\not\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}
. Since
w_{i}
in this case
is a node of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w_{i}\nVdash\theta(E_{i})
, we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w_{0}\nVdash\theta(E_{i})
.

•

\textup{Mont}({\sf par})
:
Let
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
and show

p\to A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }p\to B
for every
p\in{\sf par}
.
Let
\theta
be a substitution and
E\in{{\sf NNIL}({\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}})}
such that

\vdash E\to\theta(p\to A)
.
Hence
\vdash(E\wedge p)\to\theta(A)
. Then
by
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
we have
\vdash(E\wedge p)\to\theta(B)
and hence
\vdash E\to\theta(p\to B)
, as desired.

•

Disj:
Let
B\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }A
and

C\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }A
and show
B\vee C\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }A
.

Section

3.5
and
B\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }A
and

C\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }A
imply
B\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }A
and

C\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }A
.
Let
E\in{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}})
and
\theta
a substitution
such that
\vdash E\to\theta(B\vee C)
.
Since
E
is
{\sf IPC}
-prime,
either we have
\vdash E\to\theta(B)

or
\vdash E\to\theta(C)
. In either of the cases,
by
B\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }A
and
C\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }A
we have

\vdash E\to\theta(A)
.
So by this argument we may conclude that
(B\vee C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }A
and then
by
Section

3.5
we have
(B\vee C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }A
.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

A\rhd B
implies
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
.

Proof.

Use
sections

4.1
and
3.5
.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

For every
A\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
and
B\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
,
if
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
then

\vdash A\to B
.

Proof.

Let
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
. Then by

section

4.1
, we have

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
. Let
\theta
be identity substitution. Then we have
\vdash A\to\theta(A)
. Hence
\vdash A\to\theta(B)
, which implies

\vdash A\to B
, as desired.
∎

4.2
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-models

Before we define
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-models, the Kripke models for
which
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
is sound and complete, let us present some
definitions.
Let
\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
is a Kripke model, possibly infinite or
not tree. All over the rest of this subsection we assume that
in general a Kripke model might be infinite or not tree.
Given a set
\Gamma
of propositions,
two nodes
v,w\in W
are called
\Gamma
-similar
,
notation
v\equiv_{\Gamma}w
, if for every
A\in\Gamma

we have
\mathcal{K},v\Vdash A
iff
\mathcal{K},w\Vdash A
.
Let
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
is a set of nodes and
w\in W
.
The notation
w\preccurlyeq W^{\prime}
, means
w\preccurlyeq w^{\prime}
for every

w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
.
We say that

w\in W
is a
tight predecessor
of
W^{\prime}
, if

w\preccurlyeq W^{\prime}
and for every
u\succcurlyeq w
,
either
u=w
or
u\succcurlyeq v
for some
v\in W^{\prime}
.
A node
w
is called a
base
, if for every
finite set
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
such that
w\preccurlyeq W^{\prime}
, there
is some
w^{\prime}\in W
such that:

w\preccurlyeq w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq W^{\prime}
and
w\equiv_{\sf par}w^{\prime}
and

w^{\prime}
is a tight predecessor of
W^{\prime}
. And finally,
a Kripke model
\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
is an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model if
it is rooted (let
w_{0}
be its root) and
there is some set
W_{b}\subseteq W
with the following
properties:

•

w_{0}\in W_{b}
,

•

every
w\in W_{b}
is a base,

•

for every
w^{\prime}\in W_{b}
and
w\succcurlyeq w^{\prime}
,
there is some
v\in W_{b}
such that
v\equiv_{\sf par}w
and

w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq v\preccurlyeq w
.

Such
W_{b}
is called a
base-set
for
\mathcal{K}
.

We say that
\mathcal{K}
is
good
, if for every
finite set of nodes
W^{\prime}
, and every

X\subseteq{\sf par}

such that
\mathcal{K},W^{\prime}\Vdash X
,
there is some
w^{\prime}\in W_{b}
such that
w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq W^{\prime}
and

\mathcal{K}(w^{\prime})\cap{\sf par}=X
.

Remark 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
is an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model
with a base-set
W_{b}
, and
w\in W_{b}
.
Then
\mathcal{K}_{w}
is also an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model with the
base-set
W^{\prime}_{b}:=\{v\in W_{b}:v\succcurlyeq w\}
.

Theorem 4.9
.

(
Soundness
)

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
implies
\mathcal{K}\Vdash B
,
for every
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model
\mathcal{K}
with
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
.

Proof.

We use induction on the proof
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
. All
cases are trivial except for the axiom
{\sf V}^{\sf par}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}
and the
rule
Mont
. First we treat the Montagna’s rule.
As induction hypothesis, let
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A

implies
\mathcal{K}\Vdash B
, for every
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model
\mathcal{K}
.
Also let
\mathcal{K}\Vdash p\to A
for some
p\in{\sf par}

and
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model
\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
with the base-set

W_{b}
.
We will show
\mathcal{K}\Vdash p\to B
. Let
w\in W

such that
\mathcal{K},w\Vdash p
.
Since
\mathcal{K}
is an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model, there is some

w^{\prime}\in W_{b}
such that
w\equiv_{\sf par}w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w
.
Then
\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\Vdash p
and hence
\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\Vdash A
.
Observe that
\mathcal{K}_{w^{\prime}}
is also an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model and

\mathcal{K}_{w^{\prime}}\Vdash A
. Hence by induction hypothesis

\mathcal{K}_{w^{\prime}}\Vdash B
, which implies

\mathcal{K},w\Vdash B
, as desired.

Next we show

\mathcal{K}\Vdash{\sf V}^{\sf par}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}
for every
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model

\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
with the root
w_{0}
.
Let
B=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(E_{i}\to F_{i})
and

C=\bigvee_{i=n+1}^{n+m}E_{i}
.
Also assume
that
\mathcal{K},w_{0}\nVdash\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})
.
We show that

\mathcal{K},w_{0}\nVdash B\to C
.
By definition of
\{B\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})
, for every

E_{i}\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}
, we have

\mathcal{K},w_{0}\nVdash E_{i}
,
and for every
E_{i}\not\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}
, there is
some
w_{i}\succcurlyeq w_{0}

such that
\mathcal{K},w_{i}\Vdash B
and
\mathcal{K},w_{i}\nVdash E_{i}
.
Let
W^{\prime}:=\{w_{i}:E_{i}\not\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}\}
. There
is some
w^{\prime}\in W_{b}
which is a tight predecessor
of
W^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}\equiv_{\sf par}w_{0}
. We show that
\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash B\to C
by showing
\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\Vdash B
and

\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash C
. Let
E_{i}
be some disjunct in
C
.
If
E_{i}\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}
, then since
w^{\prime}\equiv_{\sf par}w_{0}

and
\mathcal{K},w_{0}\nVdash E_{i}
, we have

\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash E_{i}
. Otherwise, since

\mathcal{K},w_{i}\nVdash E_{i}
and
w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w_{i}
, we have

\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash E_{i}
. This finishes showing

\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash C
. Then we show
\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\Vdash B
.
Let
E_{i}\to F_{i}
is a conjunct in
B
. Consider some

w\succcurlyeq w^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{K},w\Vdash E_{i}
.
Since
w^{\prime}
is a tight predecessor
of
W^{\prime}
, either we have
w=w^{\prime}
or
w\succcurlyeq w_{j}
for
some
w_{j}\in W^{\prime}
. If
w\succcurlyeq w_{j}
, since

\mathcal{K},w_{j}\Vdash B
, we have
\mathcal{K},w\Vdash B
and then

\mathcal{K},w\Vdash E_{i}\to F_{i}
, whence
\mathcal{K},w\Vdash F_{i}
.
Also if
w=w^{\prime}
, then by the following argument, we have

\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash E_{i}
, a contradiction with our first
assumption
\mathcal{K},w\Vdash E_{i}
.
Finally, the argument for

\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash E_{i}
: if
E_{i}\in{\sf par}\cup\{\bot\}
,
then since
w^{\prime}\equiv_{\sf par}w_{0}

and
\mathcal{K},w_{0}\nVdash E_{i}
, we have

\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash E_{i}
. Otherwise, since

\mathcal{K},w_{i}\nVdash E_{i}
and
w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w_{i}
, we have

\mathcal{K},w^{\prime}\nVdash E_{i}
.
∎

We follow the methods in

[
Iemhoff, 2001b
]
to prove the completeness theorem.
This proof is almost identical to the one for
[
Iemhoff, 2001b
, proposition 7.2.2]
.
First some definitions and lemmas.
A set
w
of propositions is
{\sf IPC}
-saturated if

•

w\vdash A
implies
A\in w
,

•

\bot\not\in w
,

•

A\vee B\in w
implies either
A\in w
or
B\in w
.

Also
w
is called
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-saturated if
it is
{\sf IPC}
-saturated and

•

If
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
and
A\in w
, then
B\in w
.

Let
*(.)
is a property on sets of propositions.
We say that
*(.)
is an
extendible
property
if the following conditions hold:

•

If
*(w)
and

w\vdash A
, then
*(w\cup\{A\})
.

•

If
*(w\cup\{A\vee B\})
then
either
*(w\cup\{A\})
or
*(w\cup\{B\})
hold.

If also the following condition holds, we say that

*(.)
is
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendible property.

•

If
*(w)
and
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash w\rhd A
, then
*(w\cup\{A\})
.

In the above expression,
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash w\rhd A
is
a shorthand for
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash(\bigwedge_{i}B_{i})\rhd A
for some finite set
\{B_{i}\}_{i}\subseteq w
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For every extendible property
*(.)
, if
*(w)

for some set
w
of propositions holds,
there is some maximal

{\sf IPC}
-saturated
w^{\prime}\supseteq w
such that

*(w^{\prime})
. Moreover if
*(.)
is
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendible,
then
w^{\prime}
is also
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-saturated.

Proof.

Let
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots
be a list of all propositions
such that each proposition occurs infinitely often.
We define a sequence
w=w_{0}\subseteq w_{1}\subseteq w_{2}\subseteq\ldots
and then define
w^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{i}w_{i}
.

w_{n+1}:=\begin{cases}w_{n}\cup\{A_{n}\}\quad&:*(w_{n}\cup\{A_{n}\})\\
w_{n}&:\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

It can be easily proved that this
w^{\prime}
satisfies all required conditions.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

If
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\nvdash A\rhd B
, then there is some

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-saturated
w
such that
A\in w
and
B\not\in w
.

Proof.

Define the property
*(.)
as follows:

*(y):{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\nvdash y\rhd B

Then it is straightforward to observe that

*(.)
is
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendible and
*(\{A\})
holds. Hence

section

4.2
implies the desired result.
∎

Theorem 4.12
.

(
Completeness
)

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
is complete for good
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-models, i.e. if
for every good
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model
\mathcal{K}
, we have

\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
implies
\mathcal{K}\Vdash B
,
then
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
.

Proof.

As usual, we reason corapositively. Let

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\nvdash A\rhd B
.
Define the Kripke model
\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
as follows.
Since
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\nvdash A\rhd B
, by
section

4.2

there is some
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-saturated set
w_{0}
such that
A\in w_{0}
and

B\not\in w_{0}
. Then define

W:=\{w\supseteq w_{0}:w\text{ is a
${\sf IPC}$-saturated set of propositions}\}

Also define
u\preccurlyeq v
iff
u\subseteq v
. Finally define

w\mathrel{V}a
iff
a\in w
for atomic
a
. We will show that this model is a good
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model
such that
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A

and
\mathcal{K}\nVdash B
.
First note that by a standard argument,
one may easily prove by induction on the complexity
of
A
that
A\in w
iff
\mathcal{K},w\Vdash A
.
Then since
A\in w_{0}
and
B\not\in w_{0}
, we have

\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
and
\mathcal{K}\nVdash B
.
So it remains to show that
\mathcal{K}
is
a good
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model. Let
W_{b}
as follows:

W_{b}:=\{w\in W:w\text{ is ${{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,$-saturated}\}

We will show that
W_{b}
is a base-set, i.e. has
the following properties:

•

w_{0}\in W_{b}
,

•

every
w\in W_{b}
is a base,

•

for every
w^{\prime}\in W_{b}
and
w\succcurlyeq w^{\prime}
,
there is some
v\in W_{b}
such that
v\equiv_{\sf par}w
and

w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq v\preccurlyeq w
.

The first property is obvious. For the second property
we will need
{\sf V}^{\sf par}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}
and for the third one we will use
Mont’s rule.

Let
w\in W_{b}
and
w\preccurlyeq\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}
. We find
some tight predecessor
u
such that
u\equiv_{\sf par}w
and

w\preccurlyeq u\preccurlyeq\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}
.
Let
\hat{w}:=\bigcap_{i}w_{i}
and define

\Delta:=\{E\to F:E\to F\in\hat{w}\text{ and }(E\not\in\hat{w}\vee E\in{\sf par}\setminus w)\}

Define the property
*(.)
as follows:

*(y):y\vdash\bigvee_{i}A_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i}\text{ and $\forall\,i\ (p_{i}\in{\sf par})$ implies }\exists\,i\ (A_{i}\in\hat{w})\vee\exists\,i\ (p_{i}\in w).

Note that by letting the second disjunction as empty,
from
*(y)
we have

y\vdash\bigvee_{i}A_{i}

implies
\exists\,i\ (A_{i}\in\hat{w})
. Similarly and by considering the first disjunction as empty disjunction,
from
*(y)
we get

y\vdash\bigvee_{i}p_{i}

implies
\exists\,i\ (p_{i}\in w)
.
It is not difficult to observe that
*(.)
is an
extendible property.
Then we show
*(w\cup\Delta)
.
Let
w\cup\Delta\vdash\bigvee_{i}C_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i}
and
p_{i}\in{\sf par}
.
Then

w\vdash G\to(\bigvee_{i}C_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i})
in which

G=\bigwedge_{i}(E_{i}\to F_{i})
and
E_{i}\to F_{i}\in\Delta
.
Since
w\in W_{b}
, and

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash\left(G\to(\bigvee_{i}C_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i})\right)\rhd\left(\bigvee_{i}\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})\vee\bigvee_{i}\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(C_{i})\vee\bigvee_{i}\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(p_{i})\right),

we have
w\vdash\bigvee_{i}\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})\vee\bigvee_{i}\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(C_{i})\vee\bigvee_{i}\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(p_{i})
.
Since
w
is
{\sf IPC}
-saturated,
either
w\vdash\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})
or

w\vdash\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(C_{i})
or

w\vdash\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(p_{i})
, for some
i
. If

w\vdash\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})
, then

w,\Delta\vdash E_{i}

and since
w\cup\Delta\subseteq\hat{w}
, we have

E_{i}\in\hat{w}
, a
contradiction. So either we have

w\vdash\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(C_{i})
or

w\vdash\{G\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(D)
, in
which we have
w,\Delta\vdash C_{i}
or
w\vdash p_{i}
.
Hence either
C_{i}\in\hat{w}
or
p_{i}\in w
.
This finishes showing
*(w\cup\Delta)
.

Now let
u\supseteq(w\cup\Delta)
be a maximal

{\sf IPC}
-saturated set such that
*(u)
, as provided by

section

4.2
.
Then we show that
u
satisfies all required conditions:

•

w\preccurlyeq u\preccurlyeq\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}
. Since

w\subseteq u
, we have
w\preccurlyeq u
. Also from

*(u)
, we get
u\subseteq\hat{w}
and hence for every
i

we have
u\preccurlyeq w_{i}
.

•

w\equiv_{\sf par}u
.
Since
w\subseteq u
, we have

w\cap{\sf par}\subseteq u\cap{\sf par}
. For the other direction, let
p\in{\sf par}\cap u
. Then

u\vdash p
and from
*(u)
we have
p\in w
.

•

u
is a tight predecessor of
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}
.
We reason by contraposition.
Let
v\supsetneqq u
such that for every
i
,

w_{i}\not\subseteq v
.
Then for every
i
there is some

C_{i}\in w_{i}\setminus v
and hence

\bigvee C_{i}\in\hat{w}\setminus v
.
On the other hand,
since
u
is a maximal saturated set with

*(u)
and
v\supsetneqq u
and
v
is
{\sf IPC}
-saturated,
we have
\neg*(v)
. Hence

v\vdash\bigvee_{i}A_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i}
and
for every
i
we have
p_{i}\in{\sf par}
and
A_{i}\not\in\hat{w}
and
p_{i}\not\in w
. From

v\vdash\bigvee_{i}A_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i}
,
there is some
E
such that
either we have
E\in v\setminus\hat{w}
or

E\in{\sf par}\setminus w
. In
either of the cases, by definition of
\Delta

we have
E\to\bigvee C_{i}\in\Delta
. Hence

E\to\bigvee C_{i}\in v
and
then
\bigvee C_{i}\in v
, a contradiction.

It finishes showing the second property
of base-set
W_{b}
. Next we show that
W_{b}
satisfies
the third condition.
Let
w^{\prime}\in W_{b}
and
w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w
.
Define the property
*(.)
as follows.

*(y):\text{ for every $C$, if }{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd C\text{, then }C\in w.

We show that
*(.)
is an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendible property and

*(w^{\prime}\cup w_{\sf par})
, in which

w_{\sf par}:=w\cap{\sf par}
. First let us show why
this finishes the proof. From
section

4.2

we get some
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-saturated

v\supseteq(w^{\prime}\cup w_{\sf par})
such that
*(v)
.
Hence by definition
v\in W_{b}
.
Since
v\supseteq w^{\prime}
, we have
w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq v
.
Then we show
v\preccurlyeq w
. Let
C\in v
. From
*(v)

and
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash v\rhd C
, we have
C\in w
, as desired.
So we have
v\preccurlyeq w
. Finally we show
v\equiv_{\sf par}w
.
We must show
v_{\sf par}=w_{\sf par}
, which holds
because
v\supseteq w_{\sf par}
and
v\subseteq w
.

So it remains to show that
*(.)
is an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendible
property and
*(w^{\prime}\cup w_{\sf par})
. First we show that

*(.)
satisfies all required conditions for

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendibility:

•

If
*(y)
and
y\vdash E
. We must show

*(y\cup\{E\})
. Let
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{E\}\rhd C
.
Hence
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash E\wedge\bigwedge_{i}F_{i}\rhd C
for some
finite set
\{F_{i}\}_{i}\subseteq y
. Then since

y\vdash E
, we have
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd E\wedge\bigwedge_{i}F_{i}
. Hence
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd C
.
Then from
*(y)
we have
C\in w
, as desired.

•

If neither

*(y\cup\{E\})
nor
*(y\cup\{F\})
hold, then
we show that
*(y\cup\{E\vee F\})
does not hold.
Let
C,D
such that
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{E\}\rhd C
and

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{F\}\rhd D
and
C\not\in w
and

D\not\in w
. Hence by disjunction rule,
we have
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{E\vee F\}\rhd C\vee D
. Since

w
is
{\sf IPC}
-saturated, we also have
C\vee D\not\in w
.
Hence
*(y\cup\{E\vee F\})
does not hold.

•

Let
*(y)
and
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd E
. We
must show that
*(y\cup\{E\})
. Let

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{E\}\rhd C
. Then from
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd E
we have

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd C
. Then from
*(y)
we have

C\in w
.

Finally we show that
*(w^{\prime}\cup w_{\sf par})
. Let

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash w^{\prime}\cup w_{\sf par}\rhd C
.
Hence
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash\bigwedge w_{\sf par}\wedge E\rhd C
, for some
E\in w^{\prime}
.
Then by
Mont’s rule we have
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash\bigwedge w_{\sf par}\to E\rhd\bigwedge w_{\sf par}\to C
.
Since
E\in w^{\prime}
, we have
\bigwedge w_{\sf par}\to E\in w^{\prime}

and hence by
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-saturatedness of
w^{\prime}
we have

\bigwedge w_{\sf par}\to C\in w^{\prime}
. Since
w^{\prime}\subseteq w
,
we have
\bigwedge w_{\sf par}\to C\in w
and hence

C\in w
.

It only remains to show that
\mathcal{K}
is good. Let

w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\in W
and
\hat{w}:=\bigcap_{i}w_{i}
.
Also assume that
X\subseteq\hat{w}\cap{\sf par}
.
We find some

w\in W_{b}
such that

w\subseteq\hat{w}
and
w\cap{\sf par}={X}
.
Define

*(y):\text{For every $C_{i}$ and $p_{i}\in{\sf par}$, if }{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd\bigvee_{i}C_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i}\text{, then }\exists\,i\ C_{i}\in\hat{w}\vee\exists\,i\ p_{i}\in{X}.

We show that
*(.)
is an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendible
property and
*({X})
. Then by

section

4.2
we have some
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-saturated
w

such that
{X}\subseteq w
and
*(w)
holds.
From
*(w)
it is clear that
w\subseteq\hat{w}
. Also
if
p\in{\sf par}\cap w
, then by
*(w)
we have

p\in{X}
and hence
w^{\prime}\cap{\sf par}={X}
.
Hence
w
satisfies all required conditions.
It remains only to show that
*(.)
is

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendible property and
*({X})
.
First the
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendibility of
*(.)
:

•

If
*(y)
and
y\vdash E
. We must show

*(y\cup\{E\})
. Let
C=\bigvee_{i}C_{i}\bigvee_{i}p_{i}
and

p_{i}\in{\sf par}
and

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{E\}\rhd C
.
Hence
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash E\wedge\bigwedge_{i}F_{i}\rhd C
for some
finite set
\{F_{i}\}_{i}\subseteq y
. Then since

y\vdash E
, we have
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd E\wedge\bigwedge_{i}F_{i}
. Hence
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd C
.
Then from
*(y)
we have
C_{i}\in\hat{w}

or
p_{i}\in{X}
, for some
i
.

•

If neither

*(y\cup\{E\})
nor
*(y\cup\{F\})
hold, then
we show that
*(y\cup\{E\vee F\})
does not hold.
Let
C=\bigvee_{i}C_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i}
and

D=\bigvee_{i}D_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}q_{i}
and
p_{i},q_{i}\in{\sf par}

such that
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{E\}\rhd C
and

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{F\}\rhd D
and for all
i
we have

C_{i},D_{i}\not\in\hat{w}
and

p_{i},q_{i}\not\in{X}
.
Hence by disjunction rule,
we have
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{E\vee F\}\rhd C\vee D
,
while for all
i
,
C_{i},D_{i}\not\in\hat{w}
and
p_{i},q_{i}\not\in{X}
.
Hence
*(y\cup\{E\vee F\})
does not hold.

•

Let
*(y)
and
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd E
. We
must show that
*(y\cup\{E\})
. Let

C=\bigvee_{i}C_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i}
and
p_{i}\in{\sf par}

and

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\cup\{E\}\rhd C
.
Then from
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd E
we have

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash y\rhd C
. Then from
*(y)
we have

C_{i}\in\hat{w}
or
p_{i}\in{X}

for some
i
.

It finishes showing that
*(.)
is an
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-extendible
property. Then we show
*({X})
. Let

C=\bigvee_{i}C_{i}\vee\bigvee_{i}p_{i}

and
p_{i}\in{\sf par}
and

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash{\sf par}\cap w\rhd C
.
Then by
section

4.1

we have
{X}\vdash C
.
Since
\bigwedge({X})
is extendible,
by
section

3.5

for some
i
we have

{X}\vdash C_{i}
or

{X}\vdash p_{i}
.
Since
{X}\subseteq\hat{w}
, for some
i

either we have
C_{i}\in\hat{w}
or
p_{i}\in{X}
, as desired.
∎

4.3
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-admissibility

Lemma 4.13
.

For every good
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model
\mathcal{K}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
there is some

{\sf par}
-extendible stable class of finite rooted models

\mathscr{K}
such that for every
proposition
A
with
c(A)\leq n
we have

\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
iff
\mathscr{K}\Vdash A
.

Proof.

Given a good
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model
\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
with

W_{b}\subseteq W
as its base-set, we define a stable

{\sf par}
-extendible class
\mathscr{K}
of finite
rooted Kripke models as
follows.
\mathscr{K}
includes all Kripke models

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(W^{\prime},\preccurlyeq^{\prime},\mathrel{V}^{\prime})
with the following properties:

•

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is finite rooted with tree frame.

•

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is embeddable in
\mathcal{K}
,
i.e. there is a function
f:W^{\prime}\longrightarrow W
such
that
w^{\prime}\mathrel{V}^{\prime}a
iff
f(w^{\prime})\mathrel{V}a
; and

w^{\prime}\preccurlyeq^{\prime}v^{\prime}
implies
f(w^{\prime})\preccurlyeq f(v^{\prime})
.

•

For all
A
with
c(A)\leq n
and for every

w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w^{\prime}\Vdash A

iff
\mathcal{K},f(w^{\prime})\Vdash A
.

Obviously
\mathscr{K}
is stable and
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
implies

\mathscr{K}\Vdash A
for every
A
with
c(A)\leq n
.
It remains to show:

1.

\mathscr{K}\Vdash A
implies
\mathcal{K}\Vdash A
for every

A
with
c(A)\leq n
. It is enough to show that
for a given
n
and
w_{0}\in W
, there is a
finite rooted (with the root
w^{\prime}_{0}
) tree-frame Kripke
model
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=(W^{\prime},\preccurlyeq^{\prime},\mathrel{V}^{\prime})
which is embeddable in

\mathcal{K}
with the embedding
f
such that

f(w^{\prime}_{0})=w_{0}
and for every

w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
and
A
with
c(A)\leq n
we have

\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w^{\prime}\Vdash A
iff
\mathcal{K},w\Vdash A
.
First we inductively
define sets
W_{i}
of sequences of implications

B\to C
with
c(B\to C)\leq n
,
for
0\leq i\leq n
and the function

f
from
W_{i}
to
W
.
Then let
W^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}W_{i}
.
Let
W_{0}:=\{\langle\rangle\}
and

f(\langle\rangle):=w_{0}
. Assume that we already
defined
W_{i}
and define
W_{i+1}
as follows. For
every sequence
\sigma\in W_{i}
and
implication
B\to C
with
c(B\to C)\leq n
such that

\mathcal{K},f(\sigma)\nVdash B\vee(B\to C)
, add the new node

\sigma*\langle B\to C\rangle
to
W_{i+1}
and
define
f(\sigma*\langle B\to C\rangle)=u

for some
u
such that

u\succcurlyeq f(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{K},u\Vdash B

and
\mathcal{K},u\nVdash C
.
This finishes definition of
W_{i}
and
W^{\prime}
and the
embedding
f:W^{\prime}\longrightarrow W
. Finally define

\sigma\preccurlyeq^{\prime}\gamma
iff
\sigma
is an initial segment of

\gamma
. Since there are only finitely many
inequivalent propositions
A
with
c(A)\leq n
,
one may easily observe that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is finite. The other
required properties for
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
are easy and left to the
reader.

2.

\mathscr{K}
is
{\sf par}
-extendible.
Let
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}:=\{\mathcal{K}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{K}_{n}\}\subseteq\mathscr{K}
be finite such that

\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
is a

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle p\hfil

-submodel of some

\mathcal{K}_{0}\in\mathscr{K}
and
w^{\prime}_{i}
be
the root of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
.
Let
f_{i}
be the embedding of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
in
\mathcal{K}

and
w_{i}:=f_{i}(w^{\prime}_{i})
.
Since
\mathcal{K}
is good, there is some
u\in W
such that

u\equiv_{\sf par}w_{0}
and
u\preccurlyeq w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
and
u\in W_{b}
.
Since
u
is a base, there is some tight predecessor

v\in W
for the set
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}\}
such
that
u\equiv_{\sf par}v
and
u\preccurlyeq v\preccurlyeq w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
.
Define a

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle p\hfil

-variant
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}

of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}:=\sum(\mathscr{K}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}_{0})
in this way:

\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime},w^{\prime}_{0}\Vdash a
iff
\mathcal{K},v\Vdash a
, for every atomic
a
. Then it is not difficult to observe that

\mathcal{K}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{K}
.

∎

Lemma 4.14
.

If
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
and
\mathscr{K}\subseteq\textup{Mod}(A)
is
{\sf par}
-extendible
and stable, then
\mathscr{K}\Vdash B
.

Proof.

Let
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
and
\mathscr{K}
is a stable class of
finite rooted models with tree frames.
Let
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\mathscr{K}
to the atomics
appeared in
A,B,{\sf par}
. Obviously
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\subseteq\textup{Mod}(A)
also
is a
{\sf par}
-extendible
stable class.
Let
n:=\max\{c(A),\#{\sf par}\}
. Then

section

3.4
implies that

\langle\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\rangle_{n}
is also a

{\sf par}
-extendible stable class of finite rooted models
with tree frames.
Section

3.4
implies

\langle\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\rangle_{n}=\textup{Mod}(C)
for some

C
with
c(C)\leq n
. Moreover, by

theorem

3.12
, there is a substitution

\theta
and
C^{\prime}\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
such that

\vdash C^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\theta(C)
and
C\vdash E\leftrightarrow\theta(E)
for every proposition
E
.
On the other hand,
section

3.4

implies
\langle\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\rangle_{n}\Vdash A
. Hence
A
is
valid in
\textup{Mod}(C)
, which implies
\vdash C\to A
.
Hence
\vdash\theta(C)\to\theta(A)
and then

\vdash C^{\prime}\to\theta(A)
. From
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
infer

\vdash C^{\prime}\to\theta(B)
, or equivalently

\vdash\theta(C\to B)
. Hence for every
\mathcal{K}
,
and of course for every

\mathcal{K}\in\langle\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\rangle_{n}
we have
\mathcal{K}\Vdash\theta(C\to B)
. Since
\mathcal{K}\Vdash C
and
\theta
is

C
-projective, we have
\mathcal{K}\Vdash C\to B
, and hence

\mathcal{K}\Vdash B
. So we may deduce

\mathcal{K}\in\langle\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\rangle_{n}\Vdash B
. Since
