\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{K}\in\langle\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\rangle_{n}
, we
also have
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}\Vdash B
. Whence
\mathscr{K}\Vdash B
, as
desired.
∎

Theorem 4.15
.

The following statements are equivalent:

1.

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
.

2.

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
.

3.

B
is valid in every
{\sf par}
-extendible
stable class of Kripke models of
A
.

4.

B
is valid in every good
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
-model of
A
.

Proof.

1\to 2
:
section

4.1
.

2\to 3
:
section

4.3
.

3\to 4
:
section

4.3
.

4\to 1
:
theorem

4.12
.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

The following rule is admissible in
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
:

A\rhd B

(
E\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
)

E\to A\rhd E\to B

.

Proof.

Since
\rhd={\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }}\ \
and

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B

E\to A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }E\to B

, we have the desired result.
∎

4.4
{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}}
-preservativity logic

In the following theorem we show that the other direction of

theorem

2.2
holds when
\Gamma={{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
:

Theorem 4.17
.

{\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }}={\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }}
.

Proof.

Theorem

2.2
implies that if

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
then
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
. For the other direction,
assume that
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
seeking to show
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
.
By
section

3.5
it is enough to show
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
.
Let
E\in{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
and substitution
\theta
such that

\vdash E\to\theta(A)
. Let
\Pi_{A}
be the
{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
-projective resolution for
A
, as guarantied by

theorem

3.35
. Since
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }\bigvee\Pi_{A}

we have
\vdash E\to\bigvee\theta(\Pi_{A})
and hence
by primality of
E
,
for some
F\in\Pi_{A}
we have

\vdash E\to\theta(F)
. On the other hand, since
\Pi_{A}

is a projective resolution for
A
we have

\vdash F\to A
. Then
by
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
we get
\vdash F\to B
. Hence

\vdash\theta(F)\to\theta(B)
, which implies

\vdash E\to\theta(B)
, as desired.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

For every
\Gamma
and a logic
{\sf T}\supseteq{\sf IPC}
which admits

\Gamma
-projective resolutions,
i.e. every
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
has a
\Gamma
-projective resolution in
{\sf T}
,
the above proof works and we have
{\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${{\sf T}}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${\Gamma}$}\end{picture}}}}={\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${{\sf T}}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${\Gamma}$}\end{picture}}}}
. Hence we have

{\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }}={\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }}
.

Remark 4.19
.

By
theorems

4.17
,
4.15
and
4.4
we may conclude that:

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash A\rhd B
iff
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ }B
iff
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.65}}\put(0.0,0.3){{\footnotesize{$\sim$}}}\end{picture}}\!$}\put(-0.3,1.2){ ${}$}\put(-0.3,-0.5){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B

iff
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B

iff
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\downarrow^{\!\!{}^{\,}}}{\sf PN}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ \ }B
.

4.5
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-preservativity logic

In this subsection we axiomatize the

{{\sf N}({\sf par})}
-preservativity and
show
{\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }}=[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}
in which

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}
is defined as
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
plus the following axiom schema
(the substitution axiom):

{\sf sub}:A\rhd\theta(A)\text{ for every substitution $\theta$ (which by default is identity on parameters)}

The main point of the axiom
{\sf sub}
is that we may annihilate
occurrences of atomic variables, and together with other axioms of

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,
we may simplify propositions to
{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
-propositions.
Before we continue with providing such simplifying algorithm,
let us define
\{A\}^{\prime}(B)
and
\{A\}^{\prime\prime}(B)
, two variants of

\{A\}_{{}_{\!{\sf parb}}}\!(B)
:

\{A\}^{\prime}(B):=\begin{cases}B\quad&:B\text{ is $\bot$ or parameter}\\
A\to B&:B\in{\sf var}\\
\{A\}^{\prime}(C)\circ\{A\}^{\prime}(D)&:B=C\circ D\text{ and }\circ\in\{\vee,\wedge\}\\
({A{\downarrow}C})\to{B}&:B=C\to D\end{cases}

\{A\}^{\prime\prime}(B):=\begin{cases}A\to\bot\quad&:B\in{\sf var}\\
\{A\}^{\prime}(B)&:\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

In which
A{\downarrow}C
indicates the replacement of
D

for every occurrence of an implication
C\to D
in
A
.

Then define the following variant of Visser rule:

{\sf V}^{\prime}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}:

B\to C\rhd\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i})
, in which

B=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(E_{i}\to F_{i})
and

C=\bigvee_{i=n+1}^{n+m}E_{i}
.

Note that
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash(B\to C)\rhd\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{i})
for
B
and
C
as defined in above lines.

Lemma 4.20
.

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash{\sf V}^{\prime}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}
.

Proof.

Let
B
and
C
as in
{\sf V}^{\prime}_{\!\text{{\sf AR}}}
. By
{\sf V}({\sf parb})
we have

(B\to C)\rhd\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}_{{}_{\!{\sf parb}}}\!(E_{i})
. Also Since
for every
A
and
B
we have

\vdash\{A\}^{\prime}(B)\to\{A\}_{{}_{\!{\sf parb}}}\!(B)
,
then
{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash(B\to C)\rhd\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{i})
. So it is enough to show for every

1\leq j\leq n+m
:

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{j})\rhd\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{j})\vee\bigvee_{j\neq i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{i}).

If
E_{j}\not\in{\sf var}
we are trivially done. So assume that

E_{j}=x\in{\sf var}
and hence
\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{j})=B\to x
.
Then by the substitution axiom
{\sf sub}

we have
[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash(B\to x)\rhd\hat{\theta}(B\to x)
, in which

\theta
is the substitution with
\theta(x)=y\vee z
and
identity elsewhere and
y,z\in{\sf var}
are fresh variables, i.e. variables not appeared in
B
and
C
.
Let
E^{\prime}_{i}:=\hat{\theta}(E_{i})
and
F^{\prime}_{i}:=\hat{\theta}(F_{i})
and
B^{\prime}:=\hat{\theta}(B)
.
Hence
[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash(B\to x)\rhd(B^{\prime}\to(y\vee z))
. On the other hand by
{\sf V}({\sf parb})
we have

{{\sf AR}_{{\sf par}}}\!\,\vdash(B^{\prime}\to(y\vee z))\rhd\bigvee_{j\neq i=1}^{n}\{B^{\prime}\}^{\prime}(E^{\prime}_{i})\vee(B^{\prime}\to y)\vee(B^{\prime}\to z)
.
Let
\alpha
,

\beta
and
\gamma
be substitutions that

\alpha(y)=\alpha(z)=x,\quad\beta(y)=\gamma(z)=\bot,\quad\beta(z)=\gamma(y)=x

and identity elsewhere. Then by
{\sf sub}
we have

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash(B^{\prime}\to z)\rhd\hat{\gamma}(B^{\prime}\to z)\quad\text{ and hence }\quad[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash(B^{\prime}\to z)\rhd(B\to\bot),

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash(B^{\prime}\to y)\rhd\hat{\beta}(B^{\prime}\to y)\quad\text{ and hence }\quad[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash(B^{\prime}\to z)\rhd(B\to\bot),

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash\{B^{\prime}\}^{\prime}(E^{\prime}_{i})\rhd\hat{\alpha}(\{B^{\prime}\}^{\prime}(E^{\prime}_{i}))\quad\text{ and hence }\quad[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash\{B^{\prime}\}^{\prime}(E^{\prime}_{i})\rhd\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{i}).

Hence
[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash(B^{\prime}\to(y\vee z))\rhd\bigvee_{j\neq i=1}^{n}\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{i})\vee(B\to\bot)
and thus
[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{j})\rhd\bigvee_{j\neq i=1}^{n}\{B\}^{\prime}(E_{i})\vee\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{j})
.
∎

Lemma 4.21
.

For every
A\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
one may effectively compute

A^{\star}\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
such that:

1.

{\sf IPC}\vdash A^{\star}\to A
,

2.

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash A\rhd A^{\star}
,

3.

{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A^{\star})\subseteq{\sf sub^{{\sf atom}}}(A)
.

Proof.

By induction on
\mathfrak{o}(A)
we define
A^{\star}
with required properties. So let us first define the complexity number

\mathfrak{o}(A)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
.

•

I(A):=\{E\to F:E\to F\in{\sf sub}(A)\}
.

•

\mathfrak{i}(A):=\max\{\#I(B):B\in I(A)\}
. (
\#B
indicates the number of elements in
B
)

•

\mathfrak{c}(A)
is defined as the number of connectives occurring
in
A
.

•

\mathfrak{v}(A)
is defined as the number of occurrences of
variables occurring in
A
.

•

\mathfrak{o}(A):=(\mathfrak{i}(A),\mathfrak{d}(A),\mathfrak{v}(A))
. Finally we order triples in
\mathbb{N}^{3}
lexicographically.

Then by induction on
\mathfrak{o}(A)
define
A^{\star}
fulfilling the required conditions in the statement of lemma.

•

A=B\wedge C
: Define
A^{\star}:=B^{\star}\wedge C^{\star}
.

•

A=B\vee C
: Define
A^{\star}:=B^{\star}\vee C^{\star}
.

•

A\in{\sf var}
: Define
A^{\star}:=\bot
. Note that to
show
[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash A\rhd A^{\star}
, here we need
the substitution axiom
{\sf sub}
.

•

A\in{\sf par}
: Define
A^{\star}:=A
.

•

A=B\to C
: We have several sub-cases:

–

B
has outer disjunction, i.e. a disjunction

E\vee F
which is not in
the scope of
\to
. Then there is some
proposition
B_{0}(x)
with the following properties:
(1)
x
is a variable not appearing in
B
,
(2)
x
occurs only once in
B
,
(3)
x
has outer occurrence in
B_{0}
,
i.e.
x
is not in the scope of arrows,
(4)
B=B_{0}[x:E\vee F]
. Then define
B_{1}:=B_{0}[x:E]
and

B_{2}:=B_{0}[x:F]
and let

A^{\star}:=(B_{1}\to C)^{\star}\wedge(B_{2}\to C)^{\star}.

–

C
has outer conjunction, i.e. a conjunction

E\wedge F
which is not in
the scope of
\to
. Then there is some
proposition
C_{0}(x)
with the following properties:
(1)
x
is a variable not appearing in
C
,
(2)
x
occurs only once in
C
,
(3)
x
has outer occurrence in
C_{0}
,
i.e.
x
is not in the scope of arrows,
(4)
C=C_{0}[x:E\wedge F]
. Then define
C_{1}:=C_{0}[x:E]
and

C_{2}:=C_{0}[x:F]
and let

A^{\star}:=(B\to C_{1})^{\star}\wedge(B\to C_{2})^{\star}.

–

B=\bigwedge^{n}_{i=1}B_{i}
and

C=\bigvee^{n+m}_{i=n+1}E_{i}
in which
every
B_{i}
and
E_{i}
is either atomic or implication.
Again we have several sub-cases:

*

B_{i}=\top
for some
i
. Remove
B_{i}
from the conjunction

B
and let
B_{0}
be the result. Then define
A^{\star}:=(B_{0}\to C)^{\star}
.

*

B_{i}=\bot
for some
i
. Then define
A^{\star}:=\top
.

*

B_{i}\in{\sf var}
for some
i
. Let
\theta
be a substitution
such that
\theta(B_{i}):=\top
and
\theta
is identity elsewhere.
Then define
A^{\star}:=(\hat{\theta}(A))^{\star}
. Note that
\mathfrak{v}(\hat{\theta}(A))<\mathfrak{v}(A)
.

*

B_{i}\in{\sf par}
for some
i
. Let
B_{0}
results in by removing
B_{i}
from the conjunction
B
and define

A^{\star}:=B_{i}\to(B_{0}\to C)^{\star}.

*

B_{i}=E_{i}\to F_{i}
, for every
1\leq i\leq n
.
Then define

A_{1}:=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}((B{\downarrow}E_{i})\to C)\quad\text{and}\quad A^{\star}:=(A_{1}\wedge\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i}))^{\star}.

For last case, we reason for the following facts:

•

\mathfrak{o}((B{\downarrow}E_{i})\to C)<\mathfrak{o}(A)

for every
0\leq i\leq n
. Note that

\mathfrak{i}((B{\downarrow}E_{i})\to C)\leq\mathfrak{i}(A)
and

\mathfrak{c}((B{\downarrow}E_{i})\to C)<\mathfrak{c}(A)
.

•

\mathfrak{o}(\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i}))<\mathfrak{o}(A)
for every

1\leq i\leq n+m
. If
E_{i}\in{\sf var}
, we have this inequality because
\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i})=B\to\bot
and hence

\mathfrak{v}(\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i}))<\mathfrak{v}(A)
. For every other case we may show
\mathfrak{i}(\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i}))<\mathfrak{i}(A)
. We refer the reader to

[
Visser, 2002
, sec. 7]
.

•

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash A\rhd A^{\star}
: Use induction hypothesis and

section

4.5
.

•

{\sf IPC}\vdash A^{\star}\to A
: By induction hypothesis,
it is enough to show
{\sf IPC}\vdash(A_{1}\wedge\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i}))\to A

for every
1\leq i\leq n+m
.
So we reason inside
{\sf IPC}
. Assume
A_{1}
and
\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i})

and
B
seeking to derive
C
. If
i>n
, then by definition we have
\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i})\to C
and we are done. Otherwise, by

A_{1}
we have
(B{\downarrow}E_{i})\to C
and hence it is enough to show
B{\downarrow}E_{i}
. Hence by
B
it is enough to show

E_{i}
, which holds by
B
and
\{B\}^{\prime\prime}(E_{i})
.∎

Theorem 4.22
.

For every
A,B
, following items are equivalent:

1.

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash A\rhd B
,

2.

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
,

3.

\vdash A^{\star}\to B
.

Proof.

We show
1\Rightarrow 2\Rightarrow 3\Rightarrow 1
:

•

1\Rightarrow 2
: By
section

4.5
.

•

2\Rightarrow 3
: Let
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
.
By
section

4.5
we have
A^{\star}\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
and

\vdash A^{\star}\to A
.
Then by
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B

we get
\vdash A^{\star}\to B
.

•

3\Rightarrow 1
:
From
\vdash A^{\star}\to B
we get

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash A^{\star}\rhd B
. Also by
section

4.5

we have
[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash A\rhd A^{\star}
and then
Cut implies desired result.∎

Lemma 4.23
.

{\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }}={\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }}
.

Proof.

Sections

3.5
and
2.5
.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash A\rhd B
implies
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }B
.

Proof.

We use induction on complexity of the proof

[\![{{\sf IPC},{\sf par}}]\!]{\sf A}\vdash A\rhd B
. All steps trivially hold except:

•

{\sf sub}
: This axiom holds because
{\sf IPC}

is closed under substitutions and
\theta(E)=E
for every
E\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}
.

•

{\sf V}({\sf atomb})
:
Section

4.5
.

•

Disj:
Let
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }C
and
B\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }C
seeking to show

A\vee B\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }C
. By
section

4.5
it is enough to show

A\vee B\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{\sf PN}({\sf par})}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }C
.
Let
E\in{\sf P}{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})
such that
\vdash E\to(A\vee B)
.
Since
E
is
{\sf IPC}
-prime, either we have

\vdash E\to A
or
\vdash E\to B
.
Then by
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }C
and
B\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }C
,
in either of the cases we have

\vdash E\to C
. ∎

Lemma 4.25
.

B\to C\mathrel{\mathrel{\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){$\mid\!\approx$}\put(-0.1,1.3){ ${}$}\put(-0.1,-0.8){ ${{{\sf N}({\sf par})}}$}\end{picture}}\ \ \ }\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})
, in which

B=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(E_{i}\to F_{i})
and

C=\bigvee_{i=n+1}^{n+m}E_{i}
.

Proof.

We reason by contraposition. Let
E\in{{\sf NNIL}({\sf par})}

be such that
\nvdash E\to(\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i}))
. Hence there is some finite rooted

\mathcal{K}=(W,\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
such that

\mathcal{K},w_{0}\Vdash E
and
\mathcal{K},w_{0}\nVdash\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+m}\{B\}_{{}_{\!{\sf par}}}(E_{i})
. Let
I
be the set of indexes
i
such that
E_{i}\in{\sf par}
or
E_{i}=\bot
.
Also let
J
be the complement of
I
. Thus
for every
i\in I
we have
\mathcal{K},w_{0}\nVdash E_{i}

and for every
j\in J
, there is some
w_{j}\succcurlyeq w_{0}

such that
\mathcal{K},w_{j}\Vdash B
and

\mathcal{K},w_{j}\nVdash E_{j}
.
Let
W^{\prime}
defined as follows:

W^{\prime}:=W\setminus\{v\in W:\neg\exists j\in J(w_{j}\preccurlyeq v)\}

and define
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}:=(W^{\prime},\preccurlyeq,\mathrel{V})
.
Then since
E\in{\sf NNIL}
,
theorem

3.5

implies
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w_{0}\Vdash E
.
Moreover, it is not difficult to observe that

\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w_{0}\Vdash B
and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w_{0}\nVdash C
.
Thus
\mathcal{K}^{\prime},w_{0}\nVdash E\to(B\to C))
and then

\nvdash E\to(B\to C)
.
∎
